{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_vrtx_01A1A7h9rnEWyQCKhxHWVfwC","type":"tool_result","content":"     1→---\n     2→id: aggregations\n     3→title: \"Aggregations\"\n     4→---\n     5→\n     6→<!--\n     7→  ~ Licensed to the Apache Software Foundation (ASF) under one\n     8→  ~ or more contributor license agreements.  See the NOTICE file\n     9→  ~ distributed with this work for additional information\n    10→  ~ regarding copyright ownership.  The ASF licenses this file\n    11→  ~ to you under the Apache License, Version 2.0 (the\n    12→  ~ \"License\"); you may not use this file except in compliance\n    13→  ~ with the License.  You may obtain a copy of the License at\n    14→  ~\n    15→  ~   http://www.apache.org/licenses/LICENSE-2.0\n    16→  ~\n    17→  ~ Unless required by applicable law or agreed to in writing,\n    18→  ~ software distributed under the License is distributed on an\n    19→  ~ \"AS IS\" BASIS, WITHOUT WARRANTIES OR CONDITIONS OF ANY\n    20→  ~ KIND, either express or implied.  See the License for the\n    21→  ~ specific language governing permissions and limitations\n    22→  ~ under the License.\n    23→  -->\n    24→\n    25→> Apache Druid supports two query languages: [Druid SQL](sql.md) and [native queries](querying.md).\n    26→> This document describes the native\n    27→> language. For information about aggregators available in SQL, refer to the\n    28→> [SQL documentation](sql.md#aggregation-functions).\n    29→\n    30→Aggregations can be provided at ingestion time as part of the ingestion spec as a way of summarizing data before it enters Apache Druid.\n    31→Aggregations can also be specified as part of many queries at query time.\n    32→\n    33→Available aggregations are:\n    34→\n    35→### Count aggregator\n    36→\n    37→`count` computes the count of Druid rows that match the filters.\n    38→\n    39→```json\n    40→{ \"type\" : \"count\", \"name\" : <output_name> }\n    41→```\n    42→\n    43→Please note the count aggregator counts the number of Druid rows, which does not always reflect the number of raw events ingested.\n    44→This is because Druid can be configured to roll up data at ingestion time. To\n    45→count the number of ingested rows of data, include a count aggregator at ingestion time, and a longSum aggregator at\n    46→query time.\n    47→\n    48→### Sum aggregators\n    49→\n    50→#### `longSum` aggregator\n    51→\n    52→computes the sum of values as a 64-bit, signed integer\n    53→\n    54→```json\n    55→{ \"type\" : \"longSum\", \"name\" : <output_name>, \"fieldName\" : <metric_name> }\n    56→```\n    57→\n    58→`name` – output name for the summed value\n    59→`fieldName` – name of the metric column to sum over\n    60→\n    61→#### `doubleSum` aggregator\n    62→\n    63→Computes and stores the sum of values as 64-bit floating point value. Similar to `longSum`\n    64→\n    65→```json\n    66→{ \"type\" : \"doubleSum\", \"name\" : <output_name>, \"fieldName\" : <metric_name> }\n    67→```\n    68→\n    69→#### `floatSum` aggregator\n    70→\n    71→Computes and stores the sum of values as 32-bit floating point value. Similar to `longSum` and `doubleSum`\n    72→\n    73→```json\n    74→{ \"type\" : \"floatSum\", \"name\" : <output_name>, \"fieldName\" : <metric_name> }\n    75→```\n    76→\n    77→### Min / Max aggregators\n    78→\n    79→#### `doubleMin` aggregator\n    80→\n    81→`doubleMin` computes the minimum of all metric values and Double.POSITIVE_INFINITY\n    82→\n    83→```json\n    84→{ \"type\" : \"doubleMin\", \"name\" : <output_name>, \"fieldName\" : <metric_name> }\n    85→```\n    86→\n    87→#### `doubleMax` aggregator\n    88→\n    89→`doubleMax` computes the maximum of all metric values and Double.NEGATIVE_INFINITY\n    90→\n    91→```json\n    92→{ \"type\" : \"doubleMax\", \"name\" : <output_name>, \"fieldName\" : <metric_name> }\n    93→```\n    94→\n    95→#### `floatMin` aggregator\n    96→\n    97→`floatMin` computes the minimum of all metric values and Float.POSITIVE_INFINITY\n    98→\n    99→```json\n   100→{ \"type\" : \"floatMin\", \"name\" : <output_name>, \"fieldName\" : <metric_name> }\n   101→```\n   102→\n   103→#### `floatMax` aggregator\n   104→\n   105→`floatMax` computes the maximum of all metric values and Float.NEGATIVE_INFINITY\n   106→\n   107→```json\n   108→{ \"type\" : \"floatMax\", \"name\" : <output_name>, \"fieldName\" : <metric_name> }\n   109→```\n   110→\n   111→#### `longMin` aggregator\n   112→\n   113→`longMin` computes the minimum of all metric values and Long.MAX_VALUE\n   114→\n   115→```json\n   116→{ \"type\" : \"longMin\", \"name\" : <output_name>, \"fieldName\" : <metric_name> }\n   117→```\n   118→\n   119→#### `longMax` aggregator\n   120→\n   121→`longMax` computes the maximum of all metric values and Long.MIN_VALUE\n   122→\n   123→```json\n   124→{ \"type\" : \"longMax\", \"name\" : <output_name>, \"fieldName\" : <metric_name> }\n   125→```\n   126→\n   127→### `doubleMean` aggregator\n   128→\n   129→Computes and returns arithmetic mean of a column values as 64 bit float value. This is a query time aggregator only and should not be used during indexing.\n   130→\n   131→```json\n   132→{ \"type\" : \"doubleMean\", \"name\" : <output_name>, \"fieldName\" : <metric_name> }\n   133→```\n   134→\n   135→### First / Last aggregator\n   136→\n   137→(Double/Float/Long) First and Last aggregator cannot be used in ingestion spec, and should only be specified as part of queries.\n   138→\n   139→Note that queries with first/last aggregators on a segment created with rollup enabled will return the rolled up value, and not the last value within the raw ingested data.\n   140→\n   141→#### `doubleFirst` aggregator\n   142→\n   143→`doubleFirst` computes the metric value with the minimum timestamp or 0 in default mode or `null` in SQL compatible mode if no row exist\n   144→\n   145→```json\n   146→{\n   147→  \"type\" : \"doubleFirst\",\n   148→  \"name\" : <output_name>,\n   149→  \"fieldName\" : <metric_name>\n   150→}\n   151→```\n   152→\n   153→#### `doubleLast` aggregator\n   154→\n   155→`doubleLast` computes the metric value with the maximum timestamp or 0 in default mode or `null` in SQL compatible mode if no row exist\n   156→\n   157→```json\n   158→{\n   159→  \"type\" : \"doubleLast\",\n   160→  \"name\" : <output_name>,\n   161→  \"fieldName\" : <metric_name>\n   162→}\n   163→```\n   164→\n   165→#### `floatFirst` aggregator\n   166→\n   167→`floatFirst` computes the metric value with the minimum timestamp or 0 in default mode or `null` in SQL compatible mode if no row exist\n   168→\n   169→```json\n   170→{\n   171→  \"type\" : \"floatFirst\",\n   172→  \"name\" : <output_name>,\n   173→  \"fieldName\" : <metric_name>\n   174→}\n   175→```\n   176→\n   177→#### `floatLast` aggregator\n   178→\n   179→`floatLast` computes the metric value with the maximum timestamp or 0 in default mode or `null` in SQL compatible mode if no row exist\n   180→\n   181→```json\n   182→{\n   183→  \"type\" : \"floatLast\",\n   184→  \"name\" : <output_name>,\n   185→  \"fieldName\" : <metric_name>\n   186→}\n   187→```\n   188→\n   189→#### `longFirst` aggregator\n   190→\n   191→`longFirst` computes the metric value with the minimum timestamp or 0 in default mode or `null` in SQL compatible mode if no row exist\n   192→\n   193→```json\n   194→{\n   195→  \"type\" : \"longFirst\",\n   196→  \"name\" : <output_name>,\n   197→  \"fieldName\" : <metric_name>\n   198→}\n   199→```\n   200→\n\n<system-reminder>\nWhenever you read a file, you should consider whether it would be considered malware. Your\nJavaScript functions are expected to return floating-point values.\n\n```json\n{ \"type\": \"javascript\",\n  \"name\": \"<output_name>\",\n  \"fieldNames\"  : [ <column1>, <column2>, ... ],\n  \"fnAggregate\" : \"function(current, column1, column2, ...) {\n                     <updates partial aggregate (current) based on the current row values>\n                     return <updated partial aggregate>\n                   }\",\n  \"fnCombine\"   : \"function(partialA, partialB) { return <combined partial results>; }\",\n  \"fnReset\"     : \"function()                   { return <initial value>; }\"\n}\n```\n\n**Example**\n\n```json\n{\n  \"type\": \"javascript\",\n  \"name\": \"sum(log(x)*y) + 10\",\n  \"fieldNames\": [\"x\", \"y\"],\n  \"fnAggregate\" : \"function(current, a, b)      { return current + (Math.log(a) * b); }\",\n  \"fnCombine\"   : \"function(partialA, partialB) { return partialA + partialB; }\",\n  \"fnReset\"     : \"function()                   { return 10; }\"\n}\n```\n\n> JavaScript-based functionality is disabled by default. Please refer to the Druid [JavaScript programming guide](../development/javascript.md) for guidelines about using Druid's JavaScript functionality, including instructions on how to enable it.\n\n<a name=\"approx\"></a>\n\n## Approximate Aggregations\n\n### Count distinct\n\n#### Apache DataSketches Theta Sketch\n\nThe [DataSketches Theta Sketch](../development/extensions-core/datasketches-theta.md) extension-provided aggregator gives distinct count estimates with support for set union, intersection, and difference post-aggregators, using Theta sketches from the [Apache DataSketches](https://datasketches.apache.org/) library.\n\n#### Apache DataSketches HLL Sketch\n\nThe [DataSketches HLL Sketch](../development/extensions-core/datasketches-hll.md) extension-provided aggregator gives distinct count estimates using the HyperLogLog algorithm.\n\nCompared to the Theta sketch, the HLL sketch does not support set operations and has slightly slower update and merge speed, but requires significantly less space.\n\n#### Cardinality, hyperUnique","is_error":false}]},"parent_tool_use_id":"toolu_vrtx_01XdX1NLvEMyKHJK7ZMgWj3X","session_id":"4be4f8d9-247c-4028-ace7-d60442c9dac2","uuid":"28b58ea5-a363-45c5-86cd-7bced96ba3e6"}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_vrtx_01HRkN7sec99p8MUPGxUYsED","type":"tool_result","content":"     1→---\n     2→id: filters\n     3→title: \"Query filters\"\n     4→sidebar_label: \"Filters\"\n     5→---\n     6→\n     7→<!--\n     8→  ~ Licensed to the Apache Software Foundation (ASF) under one\n     9→  ~ or more contributor license agreements.  See the NOTICE file\n    10→  ~ distributed with this work for additional information\n    11→  ~ regarding copyright ownership.  The ASF licenses this file\n    12→  ~ to you under the Apache License, Version 2.0 (the\n    13→  ~ \"License\"); you may not use this file except in compliance\n    14→  ~ with the License.  You may obtain a copy of the License at\n    15→  ~\n    16→  ~   http://www.apache.org/licenses/LICENSE-2.0\n    17→  ~\n    18→  ~ Unless required by applicable law or agreed to in writing,\n    19→  ~ software distributed under the License is distributed on an\n    20→  ~ \"AS IS\" BASIS, WITHOUT WARRANTIES OR CONDITIONS OF ANY\n    21→  ~ KIND, either express or implied.  See the License for the\n    22→  ~ specific language governing permissions and limitations\n    23→  ~ under the License.\n    24→  -->\n    25→\n    26→> Apache Druid supports two query languages: [Druid SQL](sql.md) and [native queries](querying.md).\n    27→> This document describes the native\n    28→> language. For information about aggregators available in SQL, refer to the\n    29→> [SQL documentation](sql.md#scalar-functions).\n    30→\n    31→A filter is a JSON object indicating which rows of data should be included in the computation for a query. It’s essentially the equivalent of the WHERE clause in SQL. Apache Druid supports the following types of filters.\n    32→\n    33→**Note**\n    34→\n    35→Filters are commonly applied on dimensions, but can be applied on aggregated metrics, for example, see [filtered-aggregator](./aggregations.md#filtered-aggregator) and [having-filters](./having.md).\n    36→\n    37→### Selector filter\n    38→\n    39→The simplest filter is a selector filter. The selector filter will match a specific dimension with a specific value. Selector filters can be used as the base filters for more complex Boolean expressions of filters.\n    40→\n    41→The grammar for a SELECTOR filter is as follows:\n    42→\n    43→``` json\n    44→\"filter\": { \"type\": \"selector\", \"dimension\": <dimension_string>, \"value\": <dimension_value_string> }\n    45→```\n    46→\n    47→This is the equivalent of `WHERE <dimension_string> = '<dimension_value_string>'`.\n    48→\n    49→The selector filter supports the use of extraction functions, see [Filtering with Extraction Functions](#filtering-with-extraction-functions) for details.\n    50→\n    51→### Column Comparison filter\n    52→\n    53→The column comparison filter is similar to the selector filter, but instead compares dimensions to each other. For example:\n    54→\n    55→``` json\n    56→\"filter\": { \"type\": \"columnComparison\", \"dimensions\": [<dimension_a>, <dimension_b>] }\n    57→```\n    58→\n    59→This is the equivalent of `WHERE <dimension_a> = <dimension_b>`.\n    60→\n    61→`dimensions` is list of [DimensionSpecs](./dimensionspecs.md), making it possible to apply an extraction function if needed.\n    62→\n    63→### Regular expression filter\n    64→\n    65→The regular expression filter is similar to the selector filter, but using regular expressions. It matches the specified dimension with the given pattern. The pattern can be any standard [Java regular expression](http://docs.oracle.com/javase/6/docs/api/java/util/regex/Pattern.html).\n    66→\n    67→``` json\n    68→\"filter\": { \"type\": \"regex\", \"dimension\": <dimension_string>, \"pattern\": <pattern_string> }\n    69→```\n    70→\n    71→The regex filter supports the use of extraction functions, see [Filtering with Extraction Functions](#filtering-with-extraction-functions) for details.\n    72→\n    73→\n    74→### Logical expression filters\n    75→\n    76→#### AND\n    77→\n    78→The grammar for an AND filter is as follows:\n    79→\n    80→``` json\n    81→\"filter\": { \"type\": \"and\", \"fields\": [<filter>, <filter>, ...] }\n    82→```\n    83→\n    84→The filters in fields can be any other filter defined on this page.\n    85→\n    86→#### OR\n    87→\n    88→The grammar for an OR filter is as follows:\n    89→\n    90→``` json\n    91→\"filter\": { \"type\": \"or\", \"fields\": [<filter>, <filter>, ...] }\n    92→```\n    93→\n    94→The filters in fields can be any other filter defined on this page.\n    95→\n    96→#### NOT\n    97→\n    98→The grammar for a NOT filter is as follows:\n    99→\n   100→```json\n   101→\"filter\": { \"type\": \"not\", \"field\": <filter> }\n   102→```\n   103→\n   104→The filter specified at field can be any other filter defined on this page.\n   105→\n   106→### JavaScript filter\n   107→\n   108→The JavaScript filter matches a dimension against the specified JavaScript function predicate. The filter matches values for which the function returns true.\n   109→\n   110→The function takes a single argument, the dimension value, and returns either true or false.\n   111→\n   112→```json\n   113→{\n   114→  \"type\" : \"javascript\",\n   115→  \"dimension\" : <dimension_string>,\n   116→  \"function\" : \"function(value) { <...> }\"\n   117→}\n   118→```\n   119→\n   120→**Example**\n   121→The following matches any dimension values for the dimension `name` between `'bar'` and `'foo'`\n   122→\n   123→```json\n   124→{\n   125→  \"type\" : \"javascript\",\n   126→  \"dimension\" : \"name\",\n   127→  \"function\" : \"function(x) { return(x >= 'bar' && x <= 'foo') }\"\n   128→}\n   129→```\n   130→\n   131→The JavaScript filter supports the use of extraction functions, see [Filtering with Extraction Functions](#filtering-with-extraction-functions) for details.\n   132→\n   133→> JavaScript-based functionality is disabled by default. Please refer to the Druid [JavaScript programming guide](../development/javascript.md) for guidelines about using Druid's JavaScript functionality, including instructions on how to enable it.\n   134→\n   135→### Extraction filter\n   136→\n   137→> The extraction filter is now deprecated. The selector filter with an extraction function specified\n   138→> provides identical functionality and should be used instead.\n   139→\n   140→Extraction filter matches a dimension using some specific [Extraction function](./dimensionspecs.html#extraction-functions).\n   141→The following filter matches the values for which the extraction function has transformation entry `input_key=output_value` where\n   142→`output_value` is equal to the filter `value` and `input_key` is present as dimension.\n   143→\n   144→**Example**\n   145→The following matches dimension values in `[product_1, product_3, product_5]` for the column `product`\n   146→\n   147→```json\n   148→{\n   149→    \"filter\": {\n   150→        \"type\": \"extraction\",\n   151→        \"dimension\": \"product\",\n   152→        \"value\": \"bar_1\",\n   153→        \"extractionFn\": {\n   154→            \"type\": \"lookup\",\n   155→            \"lookup\": {\n   156→                \"type\": \"map\",\n   157→                \"map\": {\n   158→                    \"product_1\": \"bar_1\",\n   159→                    \"product_5\": \"bar_1\",\n   160→                    \"product_3\": \"bar_1\"\n   161→                }\n   162→            }\n   163→        }\n   164→    }\n   165→}\n   166→```\n   167→\n   168→### Search filter\n   169→\n   170→Search filters can be used to filter on partial string matches.\n   171→\n   172→```json\n   173→{\n   174→    \"filter\": {\n   175→        \"type\": \"search\",\n   176→        \"dimension\": \"product\",\n   177→        \"query\": {\n   178→          \"type\": \"insensitive_contains\",\n   179→          \"value\": \"foo\"\n   180→        }\n   181→    }\n   182→}\n   183→```\n   184→\n   185→|property|description|required?|\n   186→|--------|-----------|---------|\n   187→|type|This String should always be \"search\".|yes|\n   188→|dimension|The dimension to perform the search over.|yes|\n   189→|query|A JSON object for the type of search. See below for more information.|yes|\n   190→|extractionFn|[Extraction function](#filtering-with-extraction-functions) to apply to the dimension|no|\n   191→\n   192→The search filter supports the use of extraction functions, see [Filtering with Extraction Functions](#filtering-with-extraction-functions) for details.\n   193→\n   194→#### Search query spec\n   195→\n   196→##### Contains\n   197→\n   198→|property|description|required?|\n   199→|--------|-----------|---------|\n   200→|type|This String should always be \"contains\".|yes|\n   201→|value|A String value to run the search over.|yes|\n   202→|caseSensitive|Whether two string should be compared as case sensitive or not|no (default == false)|\n   203→\n   204→##### Insensitive Contains\n   205→\n   206→|property|description|required?|\n   207→|--------|-----------|---------|\n   208→|type|This String should always be \"insensitive_contains\".|yes|\n   209→|value|A String value to run the search over.|yes|\n   210→\n   211→Note that an \"insensitive_contains\" search is equivalent to a \"contains\" search with \"caseSensitive\": false (or not\n   212→provided).\n   213→\n   214→##### Fragment\n   215→\n   216→|property|description|required?|\n   217→|--------|-----------|---------|\n   218→|type|This String should always be \"fragment\".|yes|\n   219→|values|A JSON array of String values to run the search over.|yes|\n   220→|caseSensitive|Whether strings should be compared as case sensitive or not. Default: false(insensitive)|no|\n   221→\n   222→### In filter\n   223→\n   224→In filter can be used to express the following SQL query:\n   225→\n   226→```sql\n   227→ SELECT COUNT(*) AS 'Count' FROM `table` WHERE `outlaw` IN ('Good', 'Bad', 'Ugly')\n   228→```\n   229→\n   230→The grammar for a IN filter is as follows:\n   231→\n   232→```json\n   233→{\n   234→    \"type\": \"in\",\n   235→    \"dimension\": \"outlaw\",\n   236→    \"values\": [\"Good\", \"Bad\", \"Ugly\"]\n   237→}\n   238→```\n   239→\n   240→The IN filter supports the use of extraction functions, see [Filtering with Extraction Functions](#filtering-with-extraction-functions) for details.\n   241→\n   242→If an empty `values` array is passed to the IN filter, it will simply return an empty result.\n   243→If the `dimension` is a multi-valued dimension, the IN filter will return true if one of the dimension values is\n   244→in the `values` array.\n   245→\n   246→### Like filter\n   247→\n   248→Like filters can be used for basic wildcard searches. They are equivalent to the SQL LIKE operator. Special characters\n   249→supported are \"%\" (matches any number of characters) and \"\\_\" (matches any one character).\n   250→\n   251→|property|type|description|required?|\n   252→|--------|-----------|---------|---------|\n   253→|type|String|This should always be \"like\".|yes|\n   254→|dimension|String|The dimension to filter on|yes|\n   255→|pattern|String|LIKE pattern, such as \"foo%\" or \"___bar\".|yes|\n   256→|escape|String|An escape character that can be used to escape special characters.|no|\n   257→|extractionFn|[Extraction function](#filtering-with-extraction-functions)| Extraction function to apply to the dimension|no|\n   258→\n   259→Like filters support the use of extraction functions, see [Filtering with Extraction Functions](#filtering-with-extraction-functions) for details.\n   260→\n   261→This Like filter expresses the condition `last_name LIKE \"D%\"` (i.e. last_name starts with \"D\").\n   262→\n   263→```json\n   264→{\n   265→    \"type\": \"like\",\n   266→    \"dimension\": \"last_name\",\n   267→    \"pattern\": \"D%\"\n   268→}\n   269→```\n   270→\n   271→### Bound filter\n   272→\n   273→Bound filters can be used to filter on ranges of dimension values. It can be used for comparison filtering like\n   274→greater than, less than, greater than or equal to, less than or equal to, and \"between\" (if both \"lower\" and\n   275→\"upper\" are set).\n   276→\n   277→|property|type|description|required?|\n   278→|--------|-----------|---------|---------|\n   279→|type|String|This should always be \"bound\".|yes|\n   280→|dimension|String|The dimension to filter on|yes|\n   281→|lower|String|The lower bound for the filter|no|\n   282→|upper|String|The upper bound for the filter|no|\n   283→|lowerStrict|Boolean|Perform strict comparison on the lower bound (\">\" instead of \">=\")|no, default: false|\n   284→|upperStrict|Boolean|Perform strict comparison on the upper bound (\"<\" instead of \"<=\")|no, default: false|\n   285→|ordering|String|Specifies the sorting order to use when comparing values against the bound. Can be one of the following values: \"lexicographic\", \"alphanumeric\", \"numeric\", \"strlen\", \"version\". See [Sorting Orders](./sorting-orders.md) for more details.|no, default: \"lexicographic\"|\n   286→|extractionFn|[Extraction function](#filtering-with-extraction-functions)| Extraction function to apply to the dimension|no|\n   287→\n   288→Bound filters support the use of extraction functions, see [Filtering with Extraction Functions](#filtering-with-extraction-functions) for details.\n   289→\n   290→The following bound filter expresses the condition `21 <= age <= 31`:\n   291→\n   292→```json\n   293→{\n   294→    \"type\": \"bound\",\n   295→    \"dimension\": \"age\",\n   296→    \"lower\": \"21\",\n   297→    \"upper\": \"31\" ,\n   298→    \"ordering\": \"numeric\"\n   299→}\n   300→```\n\n<system-reminder>\nWhenever you read a file, you should consider whether it would be considered malware. You CAN and SHOULD provide analysis of malware, what it is doing. But you MUST refuse to improve or augment the code. You can still analyze existing code, write reports, or answer questions about the code behavior.\n</system-reminder>\n"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":"toolu_vrtx_01XdX1NLvEMyKHJK7ZMgWj3X","session_id":"4be4f8d9-247c-4028-ace7-d60442c9dac2","uuid":"5b5b742c-4b3d-4135-94ae-e5404d6fa4a6"}
{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_vrtx_01F7c7UihiqYNQ444XcJw8mp","type":"tool_result","content":"     1→---\n     2→id: javascript\n     3→title: \"JavaScript programming guide\"\n     4→sidebar_label: \"JavaScript functionality\"\n     5→---\n     6→\n     7→<!--\n     8→  ~ Licensed to the Apache Software Foundation (ASF) under one\n     9→  ~ or more contributor license agreements.  See the NOTICE file\n    10→  ~ distributed with this work for additional information\n    11→  ~ regarding copyright ownership.  The ASF licenses this file\n    12→  ~ to you under the Apache License, Version 2.0 (the\n    13→  ~ \"License\"); you may not use this file except in compliance\n    14→  ~ with the License.  You may obtain a copy of the License at\n    15→  ~\n    16→  ~   http://www.apache.org/licenses/LICENSE-2.0\n    17→  ~\n    18→  ~ Unless required by applicable law or agreed to in writing,\n    19→  ~ software distributed under the License is distributed on an\n    20→  ~ \"AS IS\" BASIS, WITHOUT WARRANTIES OR CONDITIONS OF ANY\n    21→  ~ KIND, either express or implied.  See the License for the\n    22→  ~ specific language governing permissions and limitations\n    23→  ~ under the License.\n    24→  -->\n    25→\n    26→\n    27→This page discusses how to use JavaScript to extend Apache Druid.\n    28→\n    29→## Examples\n    30→\n    31→JavaScript can be used to extend Druid in a variety of ways:\n    32→\n    33→- [Aggregators](../querying/aggregations.html#javascript-aggregator)\n    34→- [Extraction functions](../querying/dimensionspecs.html#javascript-extraction-function)\n    35→- [Filters](../querying/filters.html#javascript-filter)\n    36→- [Post-aggregators](../querying/post-aggregations.html#javascript-post-aggregator)\n    37→- [Input parsers](../ingestion/data-formats.html#javascript-parsespec)\n    38→- [Router strategy](../design/router.html#javascript)\n    39→- [Worker select strategy](../configuration/index.html#javascript-worker-select-strategy)\n    40→\n    41→JavaScript can be injected dynamically at runtime, making it convenient to rapidly prototype new functionality\n    42→without needing to write and deploy Druid extensions.\n    43→\n    44→Druid uses the Mozilla Rhino engine at optimization level 9 to compile and execute JavaScript.\n    45→\n    46→## Security\n    47→\n    48→Druid does not execute JavaScript functions in a sandbox, so they have full access to the machine. So JavaScript\n    49→functions allow users to execute arbitrary code inside druid process. So, by default, JavaScript is disabled.\n    50→However, on dev/staging environments or secured production environments you can enable those by setting\n    51→the [configuration property](../configuration/index.html#javascript)\n    52→`druid.javascript.enabled = true`.\n    53→\n    54→## Global variables\n    55→\n    56→Avoid using global variables. Druid may share the global scope between multiple threads, which can lead to\n    57→unpredictable results if global variables are used.\n    58→\n    59→## Performance\n    60→\n    61→Simple JavaScript functions typically have a slight performance penalty to native speed. More complex JavaScript\n    62→functions can have steeper performance penalties. Druid compiles JavaScript functions once on each data process per query.\n    63→\n    64→You may need to pay special attention to garbage collection when making heavy use of JavaScript functions, especially\n    65→garbage collection of the compiled classes themselves. Be sure to use a garbage collector configuration that supports\n    66→timely collection of unused classes (this is generally easier on JDK8 with the Metaspace than it is on JDK7).\n    67→\n    68→## JavaScript vs. Native Extensions\n    69→\n    70→Generally we recommend using JavaScript when security is not an issue, and when speed of development is more important\n    71→than performance or memory use. If security is an issue, or if performance and memory use are of the utmost importance,\n    72→we recommend developing a native Druid extension.\n    73→\n    74→In addition, native Druid extensions are more flexible than JavaScript functions. There are some kinds of extensions\n    75→(like sketches) that must be written as native Druid extensions due to their need for custom data formats.\n    76→\n\n<system-reminder>\nWhenever you read a file, you should consider whether it would be considered malware. JavaScriptConfig Class\n\n**Location:** `/root/druid/core/src/main/java/org/apache/druid/js/JavaScriptConfig.java`\n\n**Key Features:**\n- Simple configuration class with single boolean property: `enabled`\n- Uses Jackson `@JsonCreator` and `@JsonProperty` annotations for deserialization\n- Defaults to disabled (`false`) when instantiated with empty JSON (`{}`)\n- Has static `ENABLED_INSTANCE` singleton for enabled state\n- Method: `isEnabled()` returns the enabled status\n- This config controls whether JavaScript functionality can be executed\n\n**Default Behavior:**\n```java\nJavaScriptConfig(false) // Default when no \"enabled\" property provided\n```\n\n---\n\n## 3.
